\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\gamma\::$$\end{document} is the aggregation overhead per device (e.g., in joules or computational cycles), accounting for processing costs at the edge node (e.g., verifying ZKPs, decrypting ciphertexts).

Clustering Constraints:

Proximity constraint: Devices in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{j}$$\end{document} must be within a proximity threshold to\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{E}_{j}$$\end{document}.

28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{dist(D}_{i},{E}_{j})\le\:\theta\:,\:{\forall\:D}_{i}\in\:{C}_{j}$$\end{document}

Where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{dist(D}_{i},{E}_{j})$$\end{document} is the network distance (e.g., signal strength in dBm or latency in milliseconds), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\theta\:$$\end{document} is the maximum allowable distance (e.g., −80 dBm for signal strength).

Capacity constraint: Each device must have sufficient computational capacity.

29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{cap}_{i}\ge\:\varphi\:,\:{\forall\:D}_{i}\in\:{C}_{j}$$\end{document}

Where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{cap}_{i}$$\end{document} is the computational capacity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{i}$$\end{document} (e.g., in FLOPS), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\varnothing\:$$\end{document} is the minimum required capacity (e.g., 1 MFLOPS).

Cluster size constraint: Each cluster has a maximum size to prevent overloading.

30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left|{C}_{j}\right|\le\:\text{m}\text{a}\text{x}\_size$$\end{document}

Where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\text{m}\text{a}\text{x}\_size$$\end{document} is the maximum number of devices per cluster (e.g., 100 devices).

Clustering algorithm: Assign devices to clusters using a heuristic algorithm (k-means or graph-based clustering).

31\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{j}=\left\{{D}_{i}\left|{dist(D}_{i},{E}_{j})\le\:\theta\:\:and\:{cap}_{i}\ge\:\varphi\:\:and\left|{C}_{j}\right|\le\:\:\text{m}\text{a}\text{x}\_size\right.\right\}$$\end{document}

k-means Approach: Treat edge nodes as centroids and devices as points in a feature space defined by: 32\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\overrightarrow{f}}_{i}=\left({dist(D}_{i},{E}_{j}),{cap}_{i},\:{load}_{i}\:\right)$$\end{document}

Where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{load}_{i}$$\end{document} is the current workload of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{i}$$\end{document} (e.g., buffer occupancy). Minimize the within-cluster variance:33\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\text{min}}\sum {\:_{{j = 1}}^{m} } \sum {\:_{{D_{i} \in \:C_{j} }} } \left\| {\vec{f}_{i} - \overrightarrow {{\mu \:}} _{j} } \right\|^{2}$$\end{document}

Where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\overrightarrow{\mu\:}}_{j}$$\end{document} is the centroid of cluster \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{j}$$\end{document}.

2.Graph-Based Approach: Model devices and edge nodes as a graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:G=(V,E)$$\end{document}, with vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\text{V}=\text{D}\cup\:\text{E}$$\end{document} and edges weighted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{dist(D}_{i},{E}_{j})$$\end{document}. Use spectral clustering to partition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:G$$\end{document} into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:m$$\end{document} clusters, optimizing connectivity and capacity.

Graph-Based Approach: Model devices and edge nodes as a graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:G=(V,E)$$\end{document}, with vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\text{V}=\text{D}\cup\:\text{E}$$\end{document} and edges weighted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{dist(D}_{i},{E}_{j})$$\end{document}. Dynamic update: Update clusters at time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:t+1$$\end{document} based on current network conditions.

34\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{j}\left(t+1\right)=Update\left({C}_{j}\left(t\right),\:network\_params\left(t\right)\right)$$\end{document}

Where:

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\text{n}\text{e}\text{t}\text{w}\text{o}\text{r}\text{k}\_\text{p}\text{a}\text{r}\text{a}\text{m}\text{s}\left(\text{t}\right)=\left\{{\text{d}\text{i}\text{s}\text{t}(\text{D}}_{\text{i}},{\text{E}}_{\text{j}}),{\text{c}\text{a}\text{p}}_{\text{i}},\:{\text{l}\text{o}\text{a}\text{d}}_{\text{i}},{\text{m}\text{o}\text{b}\text{i}\text{l}\text{i}\text{t}\text{y}}_{\text{i}},\:{\text{S}\text{N}\text{R}}_{\text{i},\text{j}}\:\right\}$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\text{m}\text{o}\text{b}\text{i}\text{l}\text{i}\text{t}\text{y}}_{\text{i}}$$\end{document}: is the device movement speed (e.g., in m/s),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} Step 6: Regional data aggregation and verification

The goal of Step 6 in the Q-PRADAX system is to enable edge nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{E}_{j}$$\end{document} to aggregate encrypted health data from clustered IoMT devices in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{j}$$\end{document}, verifying data integrity and authenticity using zero-knowledge proofs to ensure privacy, computational efficiency, and scalability. Each edge node processes packets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{P}_{i}\left(t\right)=\left({\:c}_{i}\left(t\right),\:{\:\pi\:}_{i}\left(t\right),{\:ID}_{i}\right)$$\end{document}, verifying ZKPs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\:\pi\:}_{i}\left(t\right)$$\end{document}with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\text{Z}\text{K}\text{P}.\text{V}\text{e}\text{r}\text{i}\text{f}\text{y}\left({pk}_{i},\:{\pi\:}_{i}\left(t\right),\:stmt\right)$$\end{document} to confirm data validity (e.g., heart rate within 40–180 bpm) and authenticity, including only valid ciphertexts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{c}_{i}\left(t\right)$$\end{document} in aggregation and discarding tampered data. The node aggregates valid ciphertexts into a compact result \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{a}_{j}$$\end{document}, using functions like sum or average, either via homomorphic properties of Ring-LWE PQC or by decrypting with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{sk}_{j}$$\end{document}, aggregating plaintexts, and re-encrypting for cloud transmission, optimized with parallel processing to minimize computational cost.

The regional data aggregation and verification step in Q-PRADAX enables edge nodes to efficiently process encrypted health data, using ZKPs to ensure integrity and authenticity while reducing data volume for cloud transmission.

Step 7: Dynamic key management

The goal of Step 7 in the Q-PRADAX system is to dynamically update cryptographic keys for IoMT devices and edge nodes to ensure quantum-resilient security, adapt to network changes like device mobility or node failures, using a machine learning-driven approach to optimize key update frequency and lattice-based PQC for secure key distribution. Edge nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{E}_{j}$$\end{document} monitor network conditions for each cluster \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{j}$$\end{document}, triggering key updates via a decision function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:Update\:\left(t\right)$$\end{document} when changes exceed a threshold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\delta\:$$\end{document}, or based on a reinforcement learning model \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:M\left(t\right)$$\end{document} predicting optimal frequency using parameters like mobility and signal strength. New key pairs (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{pk}_{i}^{{\prime\:}},{sk}_{i}^{{\prime\:}})$$\end{document} are generated with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\text{K}\text{e}\text{y}\text{G}\text{e}\text{n}\left({1}^{ { \leftthreetimes } }\right)$$\end{document}, encrypted as (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{c}_{{k}_{i}}={\text{E}\text{n}\text{c}}_{{pk}_{i}}(pk}_{i}^{{\prime\:}},{sk}_{i}^{{\prime\:}}\left)\right)$$\end{document}, and sent to devices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{i}$$\end{document}, which decrypt them using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{sk}_{i}$$\end{document}, adopt the keys, and acknowledge receipt, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{E}_{j}$$\end{document} updates its keys and coordinates with the cloud.

Key update: For each device \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{i}\in\:{C}_{j}$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{j}$$\end{document} is the cluster managed by edge node \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{E}_{j}\in\:E$$\end{document}, generate new keys.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:M\left(t\right)$$\end{document}: A reinforcement learning model (e.g., Q-learning) with state space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:network\_params\left(t\right)$$\end{document}, action space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left\{update,\:no\_update\right\}$$\end{document}, and reward function:

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:security\:\left(t\right)$$\end{document}: Estimated security level (e.g., inversely proportional to key age).\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:overhead\:\left(t\right)$$\end{document}: Computational and communication cost of key updates.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\alpha\:,\:\beta\:$$\end{document}: Weights balancing security and efficiency.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:security\:\left(t\right)$$\end{document}: Estimated security level (e.g., inversely proportional to key age).

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:security\:\left(t\right)$$\end{document}: Estimated security level (e.g., inversely proportional to key age).

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:overhead\:\left(t\right)$$\end{document}: Computational and communication cost of key updates.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:overhead\:\left(t\right)$$\end{document}: Computational and communication cost of key updates.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\alpha\:,\:\beta\:$$\end{document}: Weights balancing security and efficiency.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\alpha\:,\:\beta\:$$\end{document}: Weights balancing security and efficiency.

The dynamic key management step in Q-PRADAX ensures quantum-resilient security by updating cryptographic keys adaptively, using PQC for secure distribution and a reinforcement learning model to optimize update frequency based on network conditions.

Step 8: Data transmission to cloud layer

The goal of Step 8 in the Q-PRADAX system is to securely transmit aggregated health data and zero-knowledge proofs from edge nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{E}_{j}$$\end{document} to the cloud for global processing, ensuring quantum-resilient security, privacy, and efficiency while minimizing communication overhead. Each edge node prepares a packet \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{a}_{j}$$\end{document} from cluster \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{j}$$\end{document} using the cloud public key \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} The packet is sent over a secure channel established in Step 1, with adaptive modulation, compression, and retransmission protocols optimizing packet size and ensuring reliable delivery.

Step 9: Cloud-level processing and storage

The goal of Step 9 in the Q-PRADAX system is to perform global aggregation of health data from edge nodes, verify data integrity and authenticity using zero-knowledge proofs, and securely store data in the cloud for healthcare applications, ensuring quantum-resilient security, privacy, scalability, and robustness in large-scale IoMT. The cloud receives packets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{P}_{j}=\left({\:c}_{j},\:{{\Pi\:}}_{j},{\:ID}_{{E}_{j}}\right)$$\end{document} from edge nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{E}_{j}$$\end{document}, decrypts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{c}_{j}={\text{E}\text{n}\text{c}}_{{pk}_{C}}\left({a}_{j}\right)$$\end{document} using its secret key \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{sk}_{C}$$\end{document}, and verifies ZKPs in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} This step offloads computation from edge nodes, supports thousands of devices, ensures privacy via ZKPs and PQC, and protects against tampering and quantum attacks, enabling real-time health monitoring and predictive analytics while aligning with Q-PRADAX goals.

The cloud-level processing and storage step in Q-PRADAX enables global health data aggregation and secure storage, using ZKPs for integrity verification and PQC for quantum-resilient protection.

Step 10: Feedback and Continuous Optimization.

Step 10: Feedback and Continuous Optimization.

Step 10: Feedback and Continuous Optimization.

The goal of Step 10 in the Q-PRADAX system is to provide feedback from the cloud to edge nodes and IoMT devices to optimize clustering, key management, and system performance, ensuring adaptability, efficiency, scalability, and robustness in large-scale IoMT. The cloud analyzes the global aggregate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:A$$\end{document} and network parameters (e.g., mobility, signal strength, failure rates) using an optimization function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\text{O}\text{p}\text{t}\text{i}\text{m}\text{i}\text{z}\text{e}$$\end{document} employing gradient descent or reinforcement learning to compute updated parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\theta\:}^{{\prime\:}}(t+1)$$\end{document} (clustering thresholds) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\delta\:}^{{\prime\:}}(t+1)$$\end{document} (key update frequencies) that minimize a system cost function balancing clustering, communication, and computational costs. These parameters are encrypted with each edge node public key \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{pk}_{{E}_{j}}$$\end{document}, transmitted over secure channels, and decrypted by edge nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{E}_{j}$$\end{document}, which update clusters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{j}(t+1)$$\end{document} using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\theta\:}^{{\prime\:}}(t+1)$$\end{document} and adjust key update frequency \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{f}_{update}$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\delta\:}^{{\prime\:}}(t+1)$$\end{document}, notifying devices as needed.

Feedback parameters: The cloud computes updated parameters.

Where:

Optimization objective: Minimize the system-wide cost.

60\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{Cost}_{system}=\sum\:_{j=1}^{m}{\text{C}\text{o}\text{s}\text{t}}_{cluster,j}+\sum\:_{j=1}^{m}{\text{C}\text{o}\text{m}\text{m}}_{j}+{\text{C}\text{o}\text{m}\text{p}}_{C}$$\end{document}

Where:

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\text{C}\text{o}\text{s}\text{t}}_{cluster,j}$$\end{document}: Clustering cost for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{j}$$\end{document}, measuring intra-cluster communication and computational overhead.

61\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\text{C}\text{o}\text{s}\text{t}}_{cluster,j}=\sum\:_{{D}_{i}\in\:{C}_{j}}({w}_{1}\cdot\:{d}_{i,j}+{w}_{2}\cdot\:{load}_{i})$$\end{document}

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{d}_{i,j}$$\end{document}: Distance (physical or signal-based) between device \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{i}$$\end{document} and edge node \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{E}_{j}$$\end{document}.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{load}_{i}$$\end{document}: Computational load of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{i}$$\end{document}.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{w}_{1},\:{w}_{2}$$\end{document}: Weights (e.g., normalized to sum to 1).

Table: symbols and Meanings.

Security analysis of Q-PRADAX

This section evaluates the security of the Q-PRADAX system against the specified adversary model in Sect. 3, focusing on its resilience to key threats in Internet of Medical Things: eavesdropping, data tampering, collusion attacks, quantum attacks, and denial-of-service (DoS) attacks. Table 3 summarizes the security analysis of Q-PRADAX.

Eavesdropping security analysis

The Q-PRADAX system robustly defends against eavesdropping attacks, where adversaries attempt to intercept encrypted health data, cryptographic keys, or feedback parameters during transmission between IoMT devices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{(D}_{i})$$\end{document}, edge nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{(E}_{j})$$\end{document}, and the cloud \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left(C\right)$$\end{document} to extract sensitive information like heart rate or glucose levels, presenting a formidable challenge due to its quantum-resilient, multi-layered cryptographic defenses. Q-PRADAX employs Ring-LWE-based post-quantum cryptography for encryption in Steps 3 (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\:c}_{i}\left(t\right)={\text{E}\text{n}\text{c}}_{{pk}_{j}}\left({\:x}_{i}\left(t\right)\right)$$\end{document}), step 8 (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{c}_{j}={\text{E}\text{n}\text{c}}_{{pk}_{C}}\left({a}_{j}\right)$$\end{document}), and step 10 (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{c}_{\theta\:,j}={\text{E}\text{n}\text{c}}_{{pk}_{{E}_{j}}}({\theta\:}^{{\prime\:}}\left(t+1\right),\:{\delta\:}^{{\prime\:}}\left(t+1\right))$$\end{document}), operating over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{R}_{q}={\mathbb{Z}}_{12289}\left[x\right]/({x}^{512}+1)$$\end{document} with security parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\: { \leftthreetimes } =256$$\end{document}. Secure channels using TLS 1.3 with AES-256-GCM in Steps 4 (BLE/Zigbee) and 8 (5G/Wi-Fi) provide additional encryption, while zk-SNARK zero-knowledge proofs (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\pi\:}_{i}\left(t\right)=\:\text{Z}\text{K}\text{P}.\,\text{P}\text{r}\text{o}\text{v}\text{e}({sk}_{i},$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\:x}_{i}\left(t\right),\:{ID}_{i},\:\text{s}\text{t}\text{m}\text{t})$$\end{document}) in Steps 3, 6, and 9 ensure that intercepted proofs reveal only statements like: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{x}_{i}\left(t\right)\in\:{R}_{valid}$$\end{document}.

Theorem 1

(Eavesdropping Resistance): Assuming the hardness of the Ring-LWE problem over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{R}_{q}={\mathbb{Z}}_{12289}\left[x\right]/({x}^{512}+1)$$\end{document} and the zero-knowledge property of zk-SNARKs, an eavesdropper intercepting packets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{P}_{i}\left(t\right)=\left({\:c}_{i}\left(t\right),\:{\:\pi\:}_{i}\left(t\right),{\:ID}_{i}\right)$$\end{document} where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\:c}_{i}\left(t\right)={\text{E}\text{n}\text{c}}_{{pk}_{j}}\left({\:x}_{i}\left(t\right)\right)$$\end{document}, cannot recover the plaintext health data\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\:x}_{i}\left(t\right)$$\end{document} or (e.g., heart rate) or cryptographic keys with non-negligible probability.

Proof

Q-PRADAX security against eavesdropping rests on three integrated mechanisms: (1) Ring-LWE semantic security, where ciphertexts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{c}_{i}\left(t\right)=(u,v)$$\end{document}, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:u=a\cdot\:s+{e}_{1}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v = pk_{j} \cdot \:s + e_{2} + \left\lfloor {q/p} \right\rfloor \cdot \:\:x_{i} \left( t \right)$$\end{document}, are indistinguishable from random polynomials due to the hardness of the Ring-LWE problem, equivalent to lattice problems like Shortest Vector Problem (SVP), with decryption probability \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:<{2}^{-256}$$\end{document}. (2) TLS 1.3 CPA-secure AES-256-GCM, ensuring packet confidentiality unless the session key is broken, requiring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{2}^{128}$$\end{document} operations; and (3) zk-SNARKs’ zero-knowledge property, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\pi\:}_{i}\left(t\right)$$\end{document} is simulatable without \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{x}_{i}\left(t\right)$$\end{document}, with distinguishing probability \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:<{2}^{-256}$$\end{document}, and soundness ensuring only valid proofs pass verification.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Data tampering security analysis

The Q-PRADAX framework robustly counters data tampering attacks, where adversaries attempt to alter health data (e.g., modifying heart rate values) or aggregated results during transmission or processing to mislead healthcare decisions, presenting a significant challenge to data integrity in IoMT. Q-PRADAX employs zk-SNARK zero-knowledge proofs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\pi\:}_{i}\left(t\right)$$\end{document} in Steps 3, 6, and 9 to prove data validity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{x}_{i}\left(t\right)\in\:{R}_{valid}$$\end{document}, e.g., heart rate in [40, 180] bpm) and authenticity (signed by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{i}$$\end{document}), with edge nodes (Step 6) and the cloud (Step 9) verifying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\text{Z}\text{K}\text{P}.\text{V}\text{e}\text{r}\text{i}\text{f}\text{y}\left({pk}_{i},\:{\pi\:}_{i}\left(t\right),\:stmt\right)=True$$\end{document}, discarding invalid packets. TLS/QUIC with HMAC-SHA256 in Steps 4 (BLE/Zigbee) and 8 (5G/Wi-Fi) ensures transmission integrity, while Step 9 stores data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:S=\left\{{\:(c}_{j},{\text{m}\text{e}\text{t}\text{a}\text{d}\text{a}\text{t}\text{a}}_{j})\right\}$$\end{document} with SHA-256 hashes in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\text{m}\text{e}\text{t}\text{a}\text{d}\text{a}\text{t}\text{a}}_{j}=({t}_{j},{ID}_{{E}_{j}},\:\left|{C}_{j}\right|,\:ag{g}_{type},\:hash\left({c}_{j}\right))$$\end{document} to detect modifications.

Theorem 2

(Data Tampering Resistance): Under the soundness of zk-SNARKs and the collision resistance of SHA-256, Q-PRADAX ensures that any tampered data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\:x}_{i}\left(t\right)$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\notin\:{R}_{valid}$$\end{document} d or modified packets are rejected by verification in steps 6 or 9 with non-negligible probability.

Proof

Q-PRADAX integrity protection integrates three mechanisms: (1) zk-SNARKs’ soundness ensures \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\text{Z}\text{K}\text{P}.\text{V}\text{e}\text{r}\text{i}\text{f}\text{y}\left({pk}_{i},\:{\pi\:}_{i}\left(t\right),\:stmt\right)=True$$\end{document} only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{x}_{i}\left(t\right)\in\:{R}_{valid}$$\end{document} and is signed by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{sk}_{i}$$\end{document}, with a \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:<{2}^{-256}$$\end{document}) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${ \leftthreetimes } =256$$\end{document}, rejecting tampered data (e.g., heart rate = 300 bpm) as 300 \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\notin\:$$\end{document} [40,180], (2) TLS HMAC-SHA256 detects in-transit modifications to packets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{P}_{i}\left(t\right)$$\end{document} or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{P}_{j}$$\end{document}, with collision probability \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:<{2}^{-128}$$\end{document}, triggering retransmission or rejection; and (3) SHA-256 hashes in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\text{m}\text{e}\text{t}\text{a}\text{d}\text{a}\text{t}\text{a}}_{j}$$\end{document} ensure stored \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{c}_{j}$$\end{document} remains unchanged, with collision probability \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:<{2}^{-256}$$\end{document}.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Collusion attacks security analysis

The Q-PRADAX framework robustly counters collusion attacks, where multiple compromised IoMT devices (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{i})$$\end{document} or edge nodes (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{E}_{i})$$\end{document} attempt to infer sensitive health data (e.g., reconstruct \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{x}_{i}\left(t\right)$$\end{document} such as heart rate) or disrupt aggregation by submitting invalid data, presenting a complex challenge in large-scale IoMT. Q-PRADAX employs zk-SNARK zero-knowledge proofs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left({\pi\:}_{i}\left(t\right)\right)$$\end{document} in Steps 3, 6, and 9 to ensure no data beyond validity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:({x}_{i}\left(t\right)\in\:{R}_{valid})$$\end{document} and authenticity is revealed, distributed clustering in Step 5 to confine compromised devices to clusters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{j}$$\end{document}, dynamic key updates in Step 7 with frequency \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{f}_{update}\in\:\left[\text{0.1,1}\right]$$\end{document} updates/hour, and privacy-preserving aggregation in Steps 6 and 9 to obscure individual \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{x}_{i}\left(t\right)$$\end{document}.

Theorem 3

(Collusion Resistance): Assuming the zero-knowledge property of zkSNARKs and Ring-LWE hardness, colluding devices in cluster \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{j}$$\end{document} cannot infer individual data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\:x}_{i}\left(t\right)$$\end{document} from other devices or produce invalid aggregates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{a}_{j}$$\end{document} with nonnegligible probability, due to privacy-preserving proofs and isolated clustering.

Proof

Q-PRADAX defense against collusion integrates four mechanisms: (1) zk-SNARKs’ zero-knowledge property ensures \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\pi\:}_{i}\left(t\right)=\text{Z}\text{K}\text{P}.\text{P}\text{r}\text{o}\text{v}\text{e}({sk}_{i},{\:x}_{i}\left(t\right),\:{ID}_{i},\:\text{s}\text{t}\text{m}\text{t})$$\end{document} is simulatable without revealing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \begin{document}$$\:<{2}^{-256}$$\end{document}, and even colluding devices in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{j}$$\end{document} cannot decrypt \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\:c}_{i}\left(t\right)={\text{E}\text{n}\text{c}}_{{pk}_{{E}_{j}}}\left({\:x}_{i}\left(t\right)\right)$$\end{document} without \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{sk}_{{E}_{j}}$$\end{document}, protected by Ring-LWE semantic security over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{R}_{q}={\mathbb{Z}}_{12289}\left[x\right]/({x}^{512}+1)$$\end{document}, 2) clustering constraints (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{dist\:(D}_{i},{E}_{j})\le\:\theta\:)$$\end{document} isolate compromised devices to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{j}$$\end{document}, and a compromised \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{E}_{j}$$\end{document} cannot access \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{c}_{i}\left(t\right)$$\end{document} from other clusters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{k}$$\end{document} due to lacking \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{sk}_{{E}_{k}}$$\end{document}; (3) key updates triggered by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\text{U}\text{p}\text{d}\text{a}\text{t}\text{e}\left(\text{t}\right)=1$$\end{document} if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\varDelta\:}_{network}\left(t\right)$$\end{document}= \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{w}_{1}\cdot\:mobility\:\left(t\right)+{w}_{2}\cdot\:failures\:\left(t\right)+{w}_{3}\cdot\:SN{R}_{change\left(t\right)}>\delta\:=0.1$$\end{document}, optimized by an ML model with reward \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:R\left(t\right)=\alpha\:\cdot\:security\:\left(t\right)-\beta\:\cdot\:overhead\:\left(t\right)$$\end{document}, limit compromised key validity; and (4) aggregation (e.g., sum, average) in Step 6 produces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{a}_{j}$$\end{document} that obscures individual \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \begin{document}$$\:{x}_{i}\left(t\right)$$\end{document}, preventing reverse-engineering even by colluding nodes.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Quantum attacks security analysis

The Q-PRADAX framework robustly counters quantum attacks, where adversaries wielding quantum computers attempt to break cryptographic schemes to decrypt sensitive health data (e.g., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\:x}_{i}\left(t\right)$$\end{document}, such as heart rate) or forge keys, exploiting vulnerabilities in traditional cryptography like RSA and ECC, posing a significant threat to IoMT. Q-PRADAX employs Ring-LWE-based post-quantum cryptography for all cryptographic operations, key generation (Step 1), encryption (Steps 3, 6, 8, 10), and key distribution (Step 7), using parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:q=12289,\:n=512$$\end{document}, and security parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\: { \leftthreetimes } =256$$\end{document}, alongside zk-SNARKs with quantum-resistant assumptions (e.g., knowledge of exponent) in Steps 3, 6, and 9, ensuring resilience even as TLS transitions to PQC.

Theorem 4

(Quantum Attack Resistance): Under the hardness of Ring-LWE and quantum-resistant zk-SNARK assumptions (e.g., knowledge of exponent), QPRADAX ensures that quantum adversaries cannot decrypt ciphertexts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\:c}_{i}\left(t\right)={\text{E}\text{n}\text{c}}_{{pk}_{j}}\left({\:x}_{i}\left(t\right)\right)$$\end{document}or forge valid zk-SNARK proofs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\:\pi\:}_{i}\left(t\right)$$\end{document} with non-negligible probability.

Proof

Q-PRADAX quantum resilience integrates two key mechanisms: (1) Ring-LWE security, based on lattice problems like Shortest Vector Problem (SVP) and Closest Vector Problem (CVP), remains hard for quantum computers, with no known algorithms solving Ring-LWE in polynomial time for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:q=12289,\:n=512$$\end{document}, the best quantum attacks require exponential time (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\approx\:2}^{n/2}\approx\:{2}^{256})$$\end{document} operations, matching NIST PQC standards like CRYSTALS-KYBER, ensuring ciphertexts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\:c}_{i}\left(t\right)={\text{E}\text{n}\text{c}}_{{pk}_{{E}_{j}}}\left({\:x}_{i}\left(t\right)\right)$$\end{document} in Step 3 are secure; 2) zk-SNARKs’ quantum resilience, using pairing-based cryptography or hash-based commitments with large-field parameters, upholds the knowledge of exponent assumption against quantum adversaries, with negligible forging probability \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(<{2}^{-256})$$\end{document}.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Denial-of-Service (DoS) security analysis

The Q-PRADAX framework effectively mitigates Denial-of-Service (DoS) attacks, where adversaries attempt to overload resource-constrained IoMT devices (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{i})$$\end{document} or edge nodes (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{E}_{i})$$\end{document} by triggering excessive data collection, transmission, or processing, aiming to disrupt data aggregation and healthcare operations in IoMT. Q-PRADAX employs resource constraints in Step 2 (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{i}<{C}_{max}=$$\end{document}1 FLOPS, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{E}_{i}\le\:E}_{max}=200\:mAh$$\end{document}), reliable transmission in Steps 4 and 8 using ARQ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\text{P}}_{\text{s}\text{u}\text{c}\text{c}\text{e}\text{s}\text{s}}={e}^{-\gamma\:\cdot\:{SNR}^{-1}}$$\end{document} and adaptive modulation, cluster size limits in Step 5, and dynamic updates in Steps 7 and 10 to adapt to disruptions by adjusting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{f}_{update}$$\end{document} and cluster parameters.

Theorem 5

(DoS Resistance): Q-PRADAX ensures that DoS attacks triggering excessive computation or transmission on devices (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{i})$$\end{document} or edge nodes (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{E}_{i})$$\end{document} are mitigated with high probability, leveraging resource constraints, reliable transmission, and adaptive clustering to maintain data aggregation under adversarial overload.

Proof

Q-PRADAX DoS resilience integrates four mechanisms: (1) resource constraints limit malicious triggers, as data collection in Step 2 at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{C}_{max}$$\end{document}, and transmission energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} prevents exhaustion; (2) transmission reliability ensures \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\text{P}}_{\text{s}\text{u}\text{c}\text{c}\text{e}\text{s}\text{s}}\approx\:0.99$$\end{document}, SNR=20dB, with ARQ retries and adaptive modulation (e.g., QPSK to BPSK) countering jamming; (3) cluster size limits restrict edge node processing to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\text{C}\text{o}\text{m}\text{m}}_{\text{j}}\approx\:260\:kB$$\end{document}, within \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} adaptive updates via Step 10 \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\text{O}\text{p}\text{t}\text{i}\text{m}\text{i}\text{z}\text{e}(\text{A},\:network\_params(t\left)\right)$$\end{document} minimize \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{Cost}_{system}$$\end{document}, tightening \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} Experimental setup for Q-PRADAX evaluation

This section details the experimental setup for evaluating the Q-PRADAX system, designed to rigorously assess its performance in IoMT. The evaluation is structured into three subsections: simulation environment and network configuration, Q-PRADAX simulation scenarios, the performance metrics.

Simulation environment and network configuration

The Q-PRADAX system is evaluated using the OMNeT + + 6.0.3 discrete event simulator with the INET 4.5 framework, customized to model IoMT with high accuracy, running on a high-performance computing cluster equipped with a 64-core AMD EPYC processor, 256 GB RAM, and Ubuntu 20.04 LTS operating system. The network consists of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:n$$\end{document}=1000 IoMT devices, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:m=$$\end{document}50 edge nodes, and one cloud server, with devices modeled as 32-bit ARM Cortex-M4 processors (80 MHz clock speed, 512 KB SRAM, 1 MB Flash), powered by 200 mAh batteries and equipped with BLE 5.0/Zigbee radios (10 mW transmit power, 250 kbps data rate, 10-meter range). Edge nodes are configured as Raspberry Pi 4 equivalents (1.5 GHz quad-core CPU, 4 GB RAM, 3000 mAh battery) with Wi-Fi connectivity (1 Mbps), and the cloud server is a virtualized instance with 16 vCPUs and 64 GB RAM. The network topology uses distributed clustering (Step 5), with cluster sizes limited to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left|{C}_{j}\right|\le\:100\:$$\end{document}devices, spatial constraints ensuring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:Dist({D}_{i},{E}_{j})\le\:$$\end{document}10 m, and signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) ranging from 10 to 30 dB to simulate interference from co-located networks or medical equipment. Cryptographic parameters include Ring-LWE encryption (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:q=12289,\:n=512,\:\chi\:={{\Psi\:}}_{16},\:\text{s}\text{e}\text{c}\text{u}\text{r}\text{i}\text{t}\text{y}\:\text{p}\text{a}\text{r}\text{a}\text{m}\text{e}\text{t}\text{e}\text{r}\: { \leftthreetimes } =256$$\end{document}), zk-SNARK proofs (Groth16, proof size ~ 0.3 kB, verification time ~0.5 ms), and TLS 1.3 with AES-256-GCM/HMAC-SHA256 for secure channels. Dynamic key updates (Step 7) occur at a frequency \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{f}_{update}$$\end{document} in [0.1,1] updates/hour. Device mobility is modeled at an average speed of 0.5 m/s (e.g., patient movement), with packet loss rates of 1–5% due to channel fading. Simulations run for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:T=24$$\end{document} hours capturing realistic IoMT dynamics such as intermittent connectivity and varying data rates (128-byte payloads every 1 s)25–27.

Q-PRADAX simulation scenarios

The Q-PRADAX simulation scenarios, implemented using tailored C + + OMNeT + + modules that model Steps 1–10 within the Sect. 6.1 simulation environment, evaluate the system scalability, security, and efficiency under realistic IoMT conditions across four 24-hour scenarios, testing computational efficiency, energy consumption, communication overhead, and fault tolerance with ciphertext sizes ~ 4 kB, proof sizes ~ 0.3 kB, and aggregation latency ≤ 500 ms, with data integrity ensured by SHA-256 hashes in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\text{m}\text{e}\text{t}\text{a}\text{d}\text{a}\text{t}\text{a}}_{j}=({t}_{j},{ID}_{{E}_{j}},\:\left|{C}_{j}\right|,\:ag{g}_{type},\:hash\left({c}_{j}\right))$$\end{document}. A Baseline Scenario uses a stable network (n = 1000 devices, m = 50 edge nodes, SNR = 20 dB, no attacks, data collection every Δt = 1 s for heart rate in [40, 180] bpm or glucose levels) to test aggregation functions (sum, average) in Steps 6 and 9; a High-Density Scenario scales to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:n=5000$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left|{C}_{j}\right|$$\end{document}≤ 200, with mobility (1 m/s), 2% packet loss, and 256 bytes/s data rates to assess clustering scalability; an Attack-Resilience Scenario introduces 5% compromised devices (50 \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{i}$$\end{document}), 2 rogue edge nodes (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{E}_{j}$$\end{document}), eavesdropping, tampering, collusion, and 10% jamming (SNR=10 dB) to evaluate security via zk-SNARK verification (Step 3), TLS/QUIC checks (Step 4), and key updates (Step 7); and an Energy-Constrained Scenario with 100 mAh devices, 1500 mAh edge nodes, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{f}_{update}$$\end{document}=1/hour, and 5% packet loss tests energy efficiency and resource management (Step 10), with attack detection and uptime recorded to assess resilience.
